Dynamic switching controller for bidirectional trajectory tracking in PV powered mobile robots considering all their subsystems and irradiance variations
Authors: Erik Reyes-Reyes, Julio Cesar Ordaz-Dehesa, Josué Roberto Rodríguez-Cerón, Ramón Silva-Ortigoza, Benjamin Natanael Santiago-Nogales, José Rafael García-Sánchez, Magdalena Marciano-Melchor, Gilberto Silva-Ortigoza
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
This paper addresses the challenge of bidirectional trajectory tracking in wheeled mobile robots (WMRs) powered by photovoltaic (PV) panels under variable irradiance. Unlike existing studies that simplify subsystem interactions, this work develops a hierarchical dynamic switching controller that integrates the WMR’s dynamic model with its actuators and power electronics. This hierarchical architecture explicitly accounts for the switching nature of the power converters to manage the energy flow from the PV source while ensuring precise motion control. A key contribution of this work is the achievement of bidirectional tracking through the management of bipolar motor voltages to drive wheel velocities in both directions; this addresses a problem not previously solved in the literature when the WMR dynamic model, the power electronics subsystem, and the PV source are considered as an integrated framework. The proposed dynamic switching controller is validated through realistic simulations in MATLAB/Simulink, using the Simscape library to model both the actuators and power electronics subsystems, as well as a commercial PV panel subjected to variable irradiance profiles and abrupt parametric variations in the electrical components. The results demonstrate that the controller effectively solves the bidirectional tracking task. Furthermore, a quantitative comparison is performed between the controller proposed in this paper and another reported in the literature. The obtained indicators show the superior performance and robustness of the proposed scheme. These indicators confirm the robustness and reliability of the dynamic switching controller, demonstrating its ability to maintain precise bidirectional trajectory tracking even under sudden irradiance fluctuations and simultaneous internal parameter variations.

Introduction

Robotics is a rapidly evolving field that continues to attract growing scientific interest, particularly in the area of wheeled mobile robots (WMRs). These systems have a wide range of applications, including target tracking, surveillance, patrolling, and environmental monitoring1,2. To perform effectively in such scenarios, WMRs require control algorithms capable of handling essential tasks such as regulation control, trajectory tracking, path following, and obstacle avoidance. A key strategy for achieving these tasks in an integrated and reliable way is to incorporate the WMR’s kinematic or dynamic model into the control design. This modeling-based approach offers several benefits, including higher tracking accuracy, improved robustness against disturbances, and greater adaptability to changing operating conditions3,4. In particular, dynamic modeling of WMRs and its integration into advanced control strategies remains an active and relevant research topic.

In parallel with advances in robotics, recent years have seen a global shift toward renewable energy sources, driven by the need to address sustainability challenges and reduce greenhouse gas emissions. Among the most prominent renewable sources are solar, wind, and geothermal energy. The convergence of robotics and renewable energy technologies holds significant promise, enabling the development of more sustainable, autonomous, and energy-efficient robotic systems capable of operating in diverse and often remote environments. Although several studies have explored the integration of renewable energy sources into mobile robotics5–7, challenges remain in application to WMRs. One of the main difficulties arises from the inherent fluctuations in voltage and current outputs from renewable sources. This issue is particularly evident in photovoltaic (PV) panels, where power oscillations occur due to variations in solar irradiance8. A promising strategy to mitigate these effects, as highlighted in previous works9–12, is to embed the dynamic model of the power electronics into the control algorithm. Since actuators’ performance depends directly on the quality of the power supply, this modeling approach can significantly enhance the overall behavior of the robotic system.

In this context, the present work addresses bidirectional trajectory tracking using a cascaded approach that integrates three key components: the mechanical structure, modeled through WMR dynamics; the actuators, consisting of a pair of DC motors; and the power electronics, represented by two full-bridge Buck inverters. These elements are analyzed under the incorporation of renewable energy sources, specifically a solar panel subject to irradiance variations, with the goal of advancing more efficient and sustainable robotic solutions.

State of the art

This section presents research related to trajectory tracking control in WMRs, proposed in recent years. These studies can be grouped into three main lines of investigation concerning the modeling and control of the system. The first approach is based exclusively on the mechanical structure of the mobile robot. On the other hand, a second approach incorporates actuators into the control system design. Finally, there is an approach that considers not only the mechanical structure and actuators but also the power electronics, aiming to provide a more comprehensive representation of the system and thereby achieve a better understanding of its behavior and performance. Each of these approaches can be further subdivided into two categories. The first focuses on kinematics, which examines the relationship between the robot’s linear and angular velocities without considering the forces acting on the system. The second category focuses on dynamics, which considers the forces acting on the robot, such as inertial forces and friction, that can significantly influence the motion of the robot.

Only mechanical structure

Kinematic: In the kinematic control area for trajectory tracking,13,14 ensure stability via Lyapunov theory, while15 enhance a PID control with bio-inspired tuning and state observer. Robust and adaptive strategies include observer-based \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {H}_\infty$$\end{document} control16, adaptive sliding mode control (SMC)17,18, and flatness-based feedback linearization19. Intelligent approaches incorporate fuzzy logic for gain scheduling and tracking20,21, or utilize neural network (NN) based self-tuning algorithms22. Furthermore, SMC is widely applied to achieve finite-time23 and fixed-time24 stability. Finally, predictive control implementations range from geometric and switched MPC25 to hybrid schemes with fuzzy compensation26 and PID-type iterative learning27.Dynamic: Regarding the dynamic analysis of WMRs, several studies implement nonlinear strategies such as backstepping28, passivity-based control29, and relay-switched techniques30. Others address physical limitations like torque saturation31, dynamic perturbations through fixed-time observers32, or robust force control33. Adaptive approaches are also prominent, utilizing Lyapunov analysis to validate NN-based constraints34 and velocity estimation observers35. Furthermore, intelligent control strategies frequently hybridize fuzzy logic with SMC36,37 or utilize bio-inspired optimization for controller tuning38. Notably, an advanced SMC implementation39 adopts a cascade double-loop structure to decouple kinematics from dynamics. Finally, recent predictive control schemes integrate type-3 fuzzy logic to mitigate measurement errors40.

Mechanical structure, actuators, and power electronics

Kinematic: Several studies provide a comprehensive analysis by jointly considering robot kinematics, actuator dynamics, and power electronics. This three-level hierarchical approach is established in52,53, where the latter specifically integrates a mobile application for Bézier curve trajectory generation. Similarly,54 proposes a switched architecture employing differential flatness and SMC for the actuator and power stages, respectively. Review of related work, research motivation, and main contributions

In this part, the state of the art is reviewed, followed by a presentation of the motivations behind this work and its main contributions.

As highlighted in the state of the art, the trajectory tracking problem in WMRs remains a current and relevant topic for the robotics research community. Various approaches have been proposed to address this problem, differing mainly in the extent to which subsystems are considered in the design of their control strategies. In this context, three main research directions are identified: (i) works that focus solely on modeling the mechanical structure, (ii) studies that incorporate both the mechanical structure and the actuators, and (iii) contributions that extend the modeling to include the mechanical structure, the actuators, and the power electronics. Moreover, these directions are further subdivided based on whether a kinematic or dynamic model is employed. Table 1 illustrates this structure, organizing the significant contributions within their respective modeling domains.

Table 1Summary of the state of the art for trajectory tracking in WMRs.Subsystems of a WMRKinematic modelDynamic modelMS 13–27 28–40 MS + A 41 42–51 MS + A + PE 52–57 58,59 MS: Mechanical structure; A: Actuators; PE: Power electronics.

Table 1Summary of the state of the art for trajectory tracking in WMRs.Subsystems of a WMRKinematic modelDynamic modelMS 13–27 28–40 MS + A 41 42–51 MS + A + PE 52–57 58,59 MS: Mechanical structure; A: Actuators; PE: Power electronics.

Summary of the state of the art for trajectory tracking in WMRs.

MS: Mechanical structure; A: Actuators; PE: Power electronics.

Based on Table 1, research focusing solely on the mechanical structure is predominant, encompassing a wide range of studies13–40. This is followed by approaches that integrate actuator dynamics41–51. In contrast, the comprehensive modeling of the three subsystems constituting a WMR, comprising the mechanical structure, actuators, and power electronics, remains the least explored area, represented by only a few contributions52–59. Within this underexplored line, the choice of the device for the power electronics subsystem becomes particularly relevant, as it can limit the applications of the WMR in complex tasks. For example, the works reported in52–56,58,59 employ unidirectional converters, which restrict the wheel velocities of the WMR to a single direction. In contrast,57 reports the use of an inverter in the power electronics subsystem, thereby providing bidirectionality to the WMR. However, the study is restricted to the kinematic model of the mechanical structure, without accounting for the broader range of phenomena that can only be captured through a dynamic formulation. It is worth noting that the inclusion of the power electronics subsystem also opens the possibility of supplying the robot with renewable energy sources, as demonstrated in57. However, the study does not consider the effect of variable irradiance on the PV panel. This omission constrains the evaluation of the WMR under realistic operating conditions, where fluctuations in solar irradiance are unavoidable and have a direct impact on system performance. In this context, and motivated by the aforementioned discussion, the main contributions of this work are as follows:

This investigation tackles the challenge of implementing a bidirectional trajectory controller in PV-powered WMRs operating under variable irradiance conditions.This study introduces a dynamic switching controller in a three-stage cascade architecture, which integrates information from the power electronics, actuators, and the mechanical structure from a dynamic perspective. To the best of the authors’ knowledge, this is the first work in the WMR literature that incorporates the dynamic model of the mechanical structure subsystem to address bidirectional trajectory tracking while explicitly considering the power electronics subsystem, thereby enabling a more comprehensive and physically grounded trajectory execution strategy.This work introduces a dynamic switching controller derived from the principles of leader–follower control, computed-torque, PI control, and SMC–PI scheme. The proposed architecture enables high-precision bidirectional trajectory tracking while achieving effective dynamic decoupling of WMRs. As a result, this is the first work in the WMR literature that addresses the problem of bidirectional trajectory tracking while explicitly considering the power electronics subsystem, and it does so by employing a switched control technique.The performance of the proposed dynamic switching controller is validated within a realistic simulation framework. The setup employs Simscape library blocks to model the photovoltaic panel emulator, the motor, and the inverter. Furthermore, to underscore the contribution of this work, a comparative analysis was conducted against the controller presented in58. The entire system is implemented in the MATLAB/Simulink environment, which provides a versatile platform for integrating the different subsystems and evaluating the controller’s effectiveness under variable operating conditions.The realistic simulation framework incorporates scenarios with fluctuating irradiance in the photovoltaic panel and abrupt variations in the internal electrical parameters, thereby emulating real-world variations in solar energy availability and dynamic system uncertainties. Results obtained from the closed-loop system demonstrate stable and reliable operation, even in the presence of simultaneous perturbations affecting both the parameters of the variable-voltage supply and the load components. These findings highlight the robustness of the proposed controller and its capability to ensure accurate bidirectional trajectory tracking under challenging and dynamically changing operating conditions.The structure of this work is organized as follows. The second section, entitled “Model of the WMR and its integrated subsystems”, provides a detailed description of the subsystems that compose the WMR, together with the mathematical modeling of each. The third section, “Dynamic switching controller”, outlines the design and development of the proposed controller, specifically aimed at solving the bidirectional trajectory tracking problem. The fourth section, “Overview of the realistic simulation framework and key parameters”, describes the simulation environment employed for validation, including the main parameters considered. Subsequently, the fifth section, “Realistic simulation results”, presents the realistic simulation process and discusses the results obtained. Finally, the section “Conclusions” summarizes the main findings of this research and highlights potential directions for future work.

The proposed architecture enables high-precision bidirectional trajectory tracking while achieving effective dynamic decoupling of WMRs. As a result, this is the first work in the WMR literature that addresses the problem of bidirectional trajectory tracking while explicitly considering the power electronics subsystem, and it does so by employing a switched control technique.

This work introduces a dynamic switching controller derived from the principles of leader–follower control, computed-torque, PI control, and SMC–PI scheme. The proposed architecture enables high-precision bidirectional trajectory tracking while achieving effective dynamic decoupling of WMRs. As a result, this is the first work in the WMR literature that addresses the problem of bidirectional trajectory tracking while explicitly considering the power electronics subsystem, and it does so by employing a switched control technique.

The performance of the proposed dynamic switching controller is validated within a realistic simulation framework. The setup employs Simscape library blocks to model the photovoltaic panel emulator, the motor, and the inverter. Furthermore, to underscore the contribution of this work, a comparative analysis was conducted against the controller presented in58. The performance of the proposed dynamic switching controller is validated within a realistic simulation framework. The setup employs Simscape library blocks to model the photovoltaic panel emulator, the motor, and the inverter. Furthermore, to underscore the contribution of this work, a comparative analysis was conducted against the controller presented in58. The entire system is implemented in the MATLAB/Simulink environment, which provides a versatile platform for integrating the different subsystems and evaluating the controller’s effectiveness under variable operating conditions.

The realistic simulation framework incorporates scenarios with fluctuating irradiance in the photovoltaic panel and abrupt variations in the internal electrical parameters, thereby emulating real-world variations in solar energy availability and dynamic system uncertainties. Results obtained from the closed-loop system demonstrate stable and reliable operation, even in the presence of simultaneous perturbations affecting both the parameters of the variable-voltage supply and the load components. These findings highlight the robustness of the proposed controller and its capability to ensure accurate bidirectional trajectory tracking under challenging and dynamically changing operating conditions.

Model of the WMR and its integrated subsystems

This section aims to model the study system, corresponding to a WMR of the differential type. The modeling approach decomposes the WMR into three fundamental modules that are typically present in this class of mobile robots: mechanical structure, actuators, and power electronics. This modular decomposition facilitates both the analytical formulation of the system dynamics and the subsequent integration of control strategies. The modules that compose the WMR are the following:Mechanical structure: This subsystem incorporates the dynamic model of the WMR, taking into account its differential drive configuration and the relevant kinematic and dynamic couplings. The model includes parameters such as wheel radius, axle length, mass distribution, and inertia, which are essential for accurately predicting the robot’s motion under various operating conditions.Actuators subsystem: Represented by two DC motors, each independently driving one of the robot’s wheels. These motors are modeled to capture their electromechanical behavior, including torque generation, back electromotive force, and the influence of load variations. The independent control of each motor enables differential steering, which is fundamental for trajectory tracking and maneuverability.Power electronics: Implemented through two full-bridge Buck inverters, this subsystem regulates the power delivery from the energy source to the actuators. The converters are modeled to account for switching dynamics, voltage regulation, and efficiency factors. Their inclusion in the model allows for the evaluation of control strategies that consider both mechanical and electrical constraints.The coordinated operation of these three subsystems enables the WMR to execute bidirectional trajectory tracking tasks with precision. The mechanical structure determines the physical motion capabilities, the actuation subsystem provides the necessary torque and speed control, and the power electronics ensure efficient and stable energy transfer. Furthermore, incorporating the power electronics model into the overall system representation makes it possible to assess the integration of renewable energy sources, such as PV modules. The interconnection among the subsystems in the WMR is illustrated in Fig. 1. It should be noted that, from this point onward and throughout the paper, the components associated with the right side of the WMR will be denoted by the subscript r, whereas those on the left side will be denoted by the subscript l. Each of the subsystems is discussed in detail in the following sections.

Mechanical structure: This subsystem incorporates the dynamic model of the WMR, taking into account its differential drive configuration and the relevant kinematic and dynamic couplings. The model includes parameters such as wheel radius, axle length, mass distribution, and inertia, which are essential for accurately predicting the robot’s motion under various operating conditions.

Mechanical structure: This subsystem incorporates the dynamic model of the WMR, taking into account its differential drive configuration and the relevant kinematic and dynamic couplings. The model includes parameters such as wheel radius, axle length, mass distribution, and inertia, which are essential for accurately predicting the robot’s motion under various operating conditions.

Actuators subsystem: Represented by two DC motors, each independently driving one of the robot’s wheels. These motors are modeled to capture their electromechanical behavior, including torque generation, back electromotive force, and the influence of load variations. The independent control of each motor enables differential steering, which is fundamental for trajectory tracking and maneuverability.

Actuators subsystem: Represented by two DC motors, each independently driving one of the robot’s wheels. These motors are modeled to capture their electromechanical behavior, including torque generation, back electromotive force, and the influence of load variations. The independent control of each motor enables differential steering, which is fundamental for trajectory tracking and maneuverability.

Power electronics: Implemented through two full-bridge Buck inverters, this subsystem regulates the power delivery from the energy source to the actuators. The converters are modeled to account for switching dynamics, voltage regulation, and efficiency factors. Their inclusion in the model allows for the evaluation of control strategies that consider both mechanical and electrical constraints.

Power electronics: Implemented through two full-bridge Buck inverters, this subsystem regulates the power delivery from the energy source to the actuators. The converters are modeled to account for switching dynamics, voltage regulation, and efficiency factors. Their inclusion in the model allows for the evaluation of control strategies that consider both mechanical and electrical constraints.

Mechanical structure

The mechanical structure of the WMR is modeled by first establishing its kinematic representation and subsequently extending it to its dynamic formulation. This progression allows the description of motion to evolve from purely geometric relationships to a complete representation that incorporates inertial effects and external forces. The starting point is the kinematic model, which defines the relationship between the Cartesian velocities of the robot and its linear and angular velocities, without considering the influence of forces or torques. This model is given by60:1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{bmatrix} \dot{x} \\ \dot{y} \\ \dot{\phi } \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} \cos \phi & 0 \\ \sin \phi & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \upsilon \\ \omega \end{bmatrix}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}being \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{x}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{y}$$\end{document} the components of the robot’s linear velocity in the inertial frame, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\upsilon$$\end{document} the linear velocity along the robot’s longitudinal axis, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega$$\end{document} the angular velocity around its center of rotation, P. Now, to incorporate the effects of forces and torques into the model, the Euler–Lagrange formalism is employed. This approach derives the equations of motion from the system’s total energy, expressed through the Lagrangian. However, since the WMR is a nonholonomic system, the resulting dynamic model contains terms that depend on the Lagrange multipliers. As discussed in60, these terms can be eliminated through a transformation based on the system’s kinematic constraints, which nullifies the constraint term. The outcome is the reduced dynamic model, which is compact and directly applicable:2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \bar{M}(q)\ddot{\eta } + \bar{V}(q, \dot{q})\dot{\eta } = \bar{B}\,\tau , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where the matrices of the reduced model are:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \bar{M}(q) = \begin{bmatrix} I_w + \frac{r^2}{4l^2}(ml^2+I) & \frac{r^2}{4l^2}(ml^2-I) \\ \frac{r^2}{4l^2}(ml^2-I) & I_w + \frac{r^2}{4l^2}(ml^2+I) \end{bmatrix}, \ \ \quad \ \qquad \bar{V}(q, \dot{q}) = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & \frac{r^2}{2l}m_c d \omega \\ -\frac{r^2}{2l}m_c d \omega & 0 \end{bmatrix}, \ \ \quad \qquad \bar{B} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \tau = \begin{bmatrix} \tau _r \\ \tau _l \end{bmatrix}, \ \qquad \qquad \eta = \begin{bmatrix} \varpi _r \\ \varpi _l \end{bmatrix}, \ \qquad \qquad q = \begin{bmatrix} x&y&\phi&\varpi _r&\varpi _l \end{bmatrix}^T, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}being \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{M}(q)$$\end{document} the inertia matrix, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{V}(q,\dot{q})$$\end{document} the Coriolis and centripetal matrix, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{B}$$\end{document} the input mapping matrix, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau$$\end{document} the vector of torques applied to the right and left wheels \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\tau _r, \tau _l)$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta$$\end{document} the vector of wheel angular velocities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\varpi _r, \varpi _l)$$\end{document}, and q the generalized coordinate vector containing the robot’s Cartesian position (x, y), orientation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi$$\end{document}, and wheel angular velocities, respectively. The physical parameters used in the reduced model are summarized in Table 2.
