Physical parameters used in the reduced dynamic model of the WMR.

Actuators subsystem

This part aims to describe the actuation subsystem, which consists of two DC motors, each coupled to one of the driving wheels of the WMR. The electromechanical behavior of each motor can be represented by the following model61:3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{array}{l} L_a \dfrac{di_a}{dt} + R_a i_a + k_e \varpi = \vartheta , \\ \\ J \dfrac{d\varpi }{dt} + b \varpi = k_t i_a, \end{array} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_a$$\end{document} is the armature inductance, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_a$$\end{document} is the armature resistance, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_a$$\end{document} is the armature current, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_e$$\end{document} is the back electromotive force constant, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varpi$$\end{document} is the angular velocity of the motor shaft, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta$$\end{document} is the applied armature voltage. The parameter J denotes the rotor moment of inertia, including the wheel and gear train if directly coupled, b is the viscous friction coefficient of the motor, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_t$$\end{document} is the torque constant. The first equation describes the electrical dynamics of the armature circuit, where the applied voltage is balanced by the resistive drop, the inductive drop, and the back electromotive force. The second equation represents the mechanical dynamics of the rotor, where the generated torque is opposed by viscous friction and the inertial torque associated with the rotor and attached components.

Power electronics

This part describes the power electronics subsystem, composed of two full-bridge Buck inverters that supply the necessary voltage and current to each DC motor of the WMR. The inverter regulates the motor’s armature voltage through high-frequency switching, allowing precise control of speed and torque. This topology enables bidirectional operation as well as regenerative braking, making it suitable for efficient energy management. The switched dynamic model of the full-bridge Buck inverter can be expressed as61:4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{array}{l} L \dfrac{di}{dt} + \nu = E(t)u, \\ \\ C \dfrac{d\nu }{dt} + \dfrac{\nu }{R} = i, \end{array} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}with L the inductance through which the current i flows, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu$$\end{document} the output voltage of the converter, C the output filter capacitance, and R the equivalent load resistance. The term E(t) represents the DC supply voltage provided by the energy source, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in \left\{ -1,0,1\right\}$$\end{document} is the switched control signal associated with the switching states of the full-bridge Buck converter. In this model, the first equation describes the inductor dynamics, where the voltage across the inductor is determined by the difference between the modulated input E(t)u and the output voltage \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu$$\end{document}. The second equation represents the capacitor dynamics, relating the rate of change of the output voltage to the load current and the resistive drop across R. This model captures the essential behavior of the power electronics and serves as the interface between the energy source and the actuators subsystem of the WMR.

Dynamic switching controller

A dynamic switching controller for bidirectional trajectory tracking in a differential-type WMR is presented in this section. The proposed design explicitly incorporates the dynamics of all WMR subsystems: the mechanical structure, the actuators, and the power electronics. In this framework, the mechanical structure is modeled by the dynamic equations of the differential-type WMR, the actuator subsystem consists of a pair of DC motors, and the power electronics subsystem is implemented using two full-bridge Buck inverters. Since the dynamic switching controller accounts for the three subsystems that compose the WMR, it is organized in a three-stage cascaded architecture, where the output of each stage serves as the input to the next. These stages are: Upper-stage control: This stage consists of two control layers. First, a kinematic control based on the leader–follower technique is employed to compute precise target angular velocities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _r$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _l$$\end{document} for the DC motors. These target velocities are then supplied to a dynamic controller that applies a computed-torque approach to generate the corresponding desired motor torques \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _r$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _l$$\end{document}. By ensuring that the wheels produce these torque commands, the condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\varpi _r, \varpi _l) \rightarrow (\omega _r, \omega _l)$$\end{document} is satisfied, enabling the WMR to accurately track its bidirectional trajectory, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x, y, \phi ) \rightarrow (x^*, y^*, \phi ^*)$$\end{document}.Middle-stage control: In this stage, two PI current controls, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _r$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _l$$\end{document}, are implemented in the actuators subsystem. Their purpose is to supply the armature currents required for the DC motors to produce the torques commanded by the upper-stage control, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\tau _r,\tau _l)$$\end{document}. Prior to this, the desired torques are converted into corresponding desired currents \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_{a_r}^*$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_{a_l}^*$$\end{document} through a mapping based on the motor torque constant. These desired currents serve as reference signals for the PI controls at this stage. When properly tuned, the control ensures that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(i_{a_r}, i_{a_l}) \rightarrow (i_{a_r}^*, i_{a_l}^*)$$\end{document}.Lower-stage control: In this stage, the control regulates the output voltages \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu _r$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu _l$$\end{document} of the full-bridge Buck inverters to track the desired profiles \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _r$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _l$$\end{document}, respectively; provided by the middle-stage control. To achieve this, two SMC–PI generate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{r}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{l}$$\end{document}, ensuring that the Buck inverters’ output voltages accurately follow the specified reference profiles, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\nu _r, \nu _l) \rightarrow (\vartheta _r, \vartheta _l)$$\end{document}.Consequently, by implementing the controls across the three specified stages, the bidirectional trajectory tracking task is accomplished. The interconnection between the control stages and the WMR is illustrated in Fig. 2, while the subsequent sections present the details of each control strategy and their integration.

Upper-stage control: This stage consists of two control layers. First, a kinematic control based on the leader–follower technique is employed to compute precise target angular velocities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _r$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _l$$\end{document} for the DC motors. These target velocities are then supplied to a dynamic controller that applies a computed-torque approach to generate the corresponding desired motor torques \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _r$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _l$$\end{document}. By ensuring that the wheels produce these torque commands, the condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\varpi _r, \varpi _l) \rightarrow (\omega _r, \omega _l)$$\end{document} is satisfied, enabling the WMR to accurately track its bidirectional trajectory, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x, y, \phi ) \rightarrow (x^*, y^*, \phi ^*)$$\end{document}.

Upper-stage control: This stage consists of two control layers. First, a kinematic control based on the leader–follower technique is employed to compute precise target angular velocities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _r$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _l$$\end{document} for the DC motors. These target velocities are then supplied to a dynamic controller that applies a computed-torque approach to generate the corresponding desired motor torques \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _r$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _l$$\end{document}. By ensuring that the wheels produce these torque commands, the condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\varpi _r, \varpi _l) \rightarrow (\omega _r, \omega _l)$$\end{document} is satisfied, enabling the WMR to accurately track its bidirectional trajectory, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x, y, \phi ) \rightarrow (x^*, y^*, \phi ^*)$$\end{document}.

Middle-stage control: In this stage, two PI current controls, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _r$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _l$$\end{document}, are implemented in the actuators subsystem. Their purpose is to supply the armature currents required for the DC motors to produce the torques commanded by the upper-stage control, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\tau _r,\tau _l)$$\end{document}. Prior to this, the desired torques are converted into corresponding desired currents \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_{a_r}^*$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_{a_l}^*$$\end{document} through a mapping based on the motor torque constant. These desired currents serve as reference signals for the PI controls at this stage. When properly tuned, the control ensures that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(i_{a_r}, i_{a_l}) \rightarrow (i_{a_r}^*, i_{a_l}^*)$$\end{document}.

Middle-stage control: In this stage, two PI current controls, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _r$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _l$$\end{document}, are implemented in the actuators subsystem. Their purpose is to supply the armature currents required for the DC motors to produce the torques commanded by the upper-stage control, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\tau _r,\tau _l)$$\end{document}. Prior to this, the desired torques are converted into corresponding desired currents \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_{a_r}^*$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_{a_l}^*$$\end{document} through a mapping based on the motor torque constant. These desired currents serve as reference signals for the PI controls at this stage. When properly tuned, the control ensures that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(i_{a_r}, i_{a_l}) \rightarrow (i_{a_r}^*, i_{a_l}^*)$$\end{document}.

Lower-stage control: In this stage, the control regulates the output voltages \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu _r$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu _l$$\end{document} of the full-bridge Buck inverters to track the desired profiles \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _r$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _l$$\end{document}, respectively; provided by the middle-stage control. To achieve this, two SMC–PI generate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{r}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{l}$$\end{document}, ensuring that the Buck inverters’ output voltages accurately follow the specified reference profiles, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\nu _r, \nu _l) \rightarrow (\vartheta _r, \vartheta _l)$$\end{document}.

Lower-stage control: In this stage, the control regulates the output voltages \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu _r$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu _l$$\end{document} of the full-bridge Buck inverters to track the desired profiles \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _r$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _l$$\end{document}, respectively; provided by the middle-stage control. To achieve this, two SMC–PI generate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{r}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{l}$$\end{document}, ensuring that the Buck inverters’ output voltages accurately follow the specified reference profiles, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\nu _r, \nu _l) \rightarrow (\vartheta _r, \vartheta _l)$$\end{document}.

Fig. 2Block diagram of the dynamic switching controller for bidirectional trajectory tracking in closed-loop.

Block diagram of the dynamic switching controller for bidirectional trajectory tracking in closed-loop.

Upper-stage control

This part focuses on the design of a control for the mechanical structure subsystem. In this context, the upper-stage control is inspired by the kinematics-into-dynamics approach 62, which consists of two hierarchical controls. The first is a kinematic control based on the leader–follower principle, whose outputs serve as inputs to the second control. The latter is a computed-torque controller that explicitly accounts for the dynamics of the WMR.

In this context, the kinematic control is designed to track a reference WMR that follows the desired trajectory \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x^*, y^*, \phi ^*)$$\end{document}. To achieve this, a kinematic model of the reference WMR is defined, serving as the basis for generating the target motion profiles. This kinematic model captures the geometric relationships between the vehicle’s pose, its linear and angular velocities, and is given by:5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{bmatrix} \dot{x}^* \\ \dot{y}^* \\ \dot{\phi }^* \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} \cos \phi ^* & 0 \\ \sin \phi ^* & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \upsilon ^* \\ \omega ^* \end{bmatrix}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Since the WMR must track the reference mobile robot, the following tracking error equation can be derived:6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{bmatrix} e_x\\ e_y\\ e_\phi \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} \Gamma _1\cos \phi & \Gamma _1\sin \phi & 0\\ -\Gamma _1\sin \phi & \Gamma _1\cos \phi & \gamma \\ 0 & 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} x^* - x \\ y^* - y \\ \phi ^* - \phi \end{bmatrix}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}being \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma _1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma$$\end{document} positive constants. By computing the time derivative of (6) and substituting the expressions from (1) and (5), the tracking error dynamics is obtained as:7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{bmatrix} \dot{e}_x\\ \dot{e}_y\\ \dot{e}_\phi \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} -\Gamma _1 & e_y - \gamma e_\phi \\ 0 & - e_x - \gamma \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix} \upsilon \\ \omega \end{bmatrix} + \begin{bmatrix} v^*\Gamma _1\cos e_\phi \\ \Gamma _1v^*\sin e_\phi + \gamma \omega ^*\\ \omega ^* \end{bmatrix}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Building on Eq. (7) and following the approach in62, the following kinematic control law is proposed for the linear and angular velocity inputs, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\upsilon$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega$$\end{document}:8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \upsilon&= \upsilon ^*\cos e_\phi + \Gamma _2e_x, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \omega&= \omega ^* + v^*\Gamma _1e_y + \Gamma _3\sin e_\phi , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma _2$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma _3$$\end{document} are positive constants and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma > 1 / \Gamma _3$$\end{document}. The control laws in (8) and (9) are formulated in terms of the linear and angular velocities at point P. In the present case, however, the dynamic control requires the control inputs to be the angular velocities of the wheels. This can be achieved by computing the following relationship between the right and left wheel angular velocities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\omega _r, \omega _l)$$\end{document} and the linear and angular velocities at point P\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\upsilon , \omega )$$\end{document}:10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{bmatrix} \omega _r\\ \omega _l \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} \frac{r}{2} & \frac{r}{2}\\ \\ \frac{r}{2l} & -\frac{r}{2l} \end{bmatrix}^{-1} \begin{bmatrix} \upsilon \\ \omega \end{bmatrix}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}From the kinematic control laws in (8) and (9), together with the mapping in (10), smooth reference angular velocities for the wheels, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\omega _r,\omega _l)$$\end{document}, are generated. These references satisfy the robot’s nonholonomic constraints and guide the system toward the desired trajectory. Since the kinematic layer neglects mass, inertia, friction, and torque limits, the velocity references are passed to a dynamic controller. This controller computes the wheel torques \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\tau _r,\tau _l)$$\end{document} required to track the references, explicitly accounting for the chassis and wheel–motor inertias. In this way, the dynamic response remains consistent with the admissible kinematic subspace. Within this framework, the dynamic controller is formulated as a computed-torque control law, specifically designed to compensate for the nonlinear dynamics of the mobile platform, as detailed below,11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \bar{M}(q)\left( \dot{\eta }^* - \begin{bmatrix} K_{p_1} & 0 \\ 0 & K_{p_2} \end{bmatrix}\widetilde{\eta } - \begin{bmatrix} K_{i_1} & 0 \\ 0 & K_{i_2} \end{bmatrix} \int _{0}^{t} \widetilde{\eta }d\rho \right) + \bar{V}(q,\dot{q})\eta = \tau , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\eta }$$\end{document} denotes the angular velocity tracking error vector, defined as:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \widetilde{\eta } = \eta - \eta ^* = \begin{bmatrix} \varpi _r\\ \varpi _l \end{bmatrix} - \begin{bmatrix} \omega _r\\ \omega _l \end{bmatrix}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varpi _r$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varpi _l$$\end{document} representing the actual right and left wheel angular velocities, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _r$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _l$$\end{document} their respective reference values generated by the kinematic controller in (10). Next, by equating the computed-torque control law in (11) with the WMR dynamic model described in (2), the following equation is obtained:12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \dot{\widetilde{\eta }} = -\begin{bmatrix} K_{p_1} & 0 \\ 0 & K_{p_2} \end{bmatrix}\widetilde{\eta } - \begin{bmatrix} K_{i_1} & 0 \\ 0 & K_{i_2} \end{bmatrix} \int _{0}^{t} \widetilde{\eta }d\rho . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Now, by differentiating expression (12) with respect to time, the following closed-loop error dynamics is obtained:13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \ddot{\widetilde{\eta }} + \begin{bmatrix} K_{p_1} & 0 \\ 0 & K_{p_2} \end{bmatrix} \dot{\widetilde{\eta }} + \begin{bmatrix} K_{i_1} & 0 \\ 0 & K_{i_2} \end{bmatrix} \widetilde{\eta } = 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}From (13), the tracking error can be decomposed into each component of the vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\eta }$$\end{document} as follows:14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \ddot{\widetilde{\eta }}_{r} + K_{p_1}\dot{\widetilde{\eta }}_r + K_{i_1}\widetilde{\eta }_r= & 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \ddot{\widetilde{\eta }}_{l} + K_{p_2}\dot{\widetilde{\eta }}_l + K_{i_2}\widetilde{\eta }_l= & 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}being \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\eta }_r = \varpi _r - \omega _r$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\eta }_l = \varpi _l - \omega _l$$\end{document}. Thus, according to the Routh–Hurwitz stability criterion, as long as the gains \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_{p_1}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_{p_2}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_{i_1}$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_{i_2}$$\end{document} are positive, the computed-torques \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\tau _r, \tau _l)$$\end{document} will ensure that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\varpi _r, \varpi _l) \rightarrow (\omega _r, \omega _l)$$\end{document}. Consequently, bidirectional trajectory tracking will be achieved, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x, y, \phi ) \rightarrow (x^*, y^*, \phi ^*)$$\end{document}.

Middle-stage control

The design of the control scheme for the actuators subsystem is addressed in this part. The main objective of this control is to ensure that the DC motors accurately deliver the torque required by the upper-stage control. Since the torque cannot be measured directly with the available sensors, it will be regulated indirectly by controlling the armature current, which is linearly related to the developed torque through the motor torque constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_t$$\end{document}. Therefore, the first step consists on mapping the torque reference, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau$$\end{document}, generated by the upper-stage control into an equivalent current reference, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_a^*$$\end{document}, using \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_t$$\end{document}, according to:16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} i_a^* = \frac{\tau }{k_t}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau$$\end{document} is the torque profile imposed by the upper-stage control and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_a^*$$\end{document} is the corresponding reference armature current for the middle-stage control. Once the desired torque profile has been mapped into its equivalent current reference, the control problem is reformulated as a current tracking task. To address this tracking problem, the following PI current control is proposed, as in60:17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \vartheta = \alpha _1\big (i_a^* - i_a\big ) + \alpha _2 \int _{0}^{t} \big (i_a^* - i_a\big ) \, d\rho , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}being \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _2$$\end{document} the control gains. To verify that the controller in (17) fulfills its intended objective, the armature current tracking error is defined as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_{i_a} = i_a - i_a^*$$\end{document}. The Laplace transform is then applied to both the system dynamics (3) and the control law (17). Considering \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_e=k_t$$\end{document}, and after straightforward algebraic manipulation, the feedback interconnection between the current controller (17) and the actuation subsystem (3) can be expressed as:18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} e_{i_a}(s) = G_1(s)\, i_a^*(s) + G_2(s)\, \omega (s), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}with\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G_1(s) \;=\; -\frac{R_a}{\alpha _2}s \cdot \frac{s + \frac{R_a}{L_a}}{\frac{R_a}{L_a}} \cdot \frac{\frac{\alpha _2}{L_a}}{\,s^2 + \frac{R_a + \alpha _1}{L_a}\,s + \frac{\alpha _2}{L_a}\,}, \qquad G_2(s) = -\frac{k_t}{\alpha _2} \cdot \frac{\frac{\alpha _2}{L_a} \, s}{s^2 + \frac{R_a + \alpha _1}{L_a} s + \frac{\alpha _2}{L_a}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Observe that the stability of the transfer functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_1(s)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_2(s)$$\end{document}, and therefore the entire system, is guaranteed by placing all poles in the open left-half plane. This is achieved by selecting positive gains, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _2$$\end{document}. These conditions ensure that the armature current \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_a$$\end{document} asymptotically converges to its reference \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_a^*$$\end{document} as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t \rightarrow \infty$$\end{document}.

Lower-stage control

In this subsection, the design of a control strategy for the power electronics subsystem is presented. Since the power electronics subsystem consists of a full-bridge Buck inverter, the objective of this control is to ensure that the inverter output voltage \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu$$\end{document} accurately tracks its desired profile \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta$$\end{document}, as specified by the middle-stage control. To this end, associated with (4) and following the approach presented in61, the following switched control law is considered:19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u & = -\text {sign}(h), \qquad h= i-i_*,\\ \ \text {sign}(h)& = {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} +1, & \text {if} \quad h\ge 0,\\ -1, & \text {if} \quad h<0, \end{array}\right. } \\ e_\nu & =\nu ^*-\nu ,\\ i_*& = C\frac{d\nu ^*}{dt}+\frac{\nu ^*}{R} + k_pe_\nu +k_i\int ^t_0e_\nu d\rho, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu ^* = \vartheta$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_p, k_i > 0$$\end{document}. Building upon the results reported in 61, the stability of the controller described in (19) has been rigorously established. Consequently, the closed-loop system satisfies its intended control objective, namely \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu \rightarrow \nu ^*$$\end{document} as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t \rightarrow \infty$$\end{document}. This convergence ensures that the tracking error asymptotically vanishes, thereby guaranteeing the desired steady-state performance under the specified gain conditions.

Dynamic switching controller for bidirectional trajectory tracking

In this part, the dynamic switching controller for bidirectional trajectory tracking is introduced. The controller is formulated as a cascaded interconnection of the control schemes presented in the previous subsections. By integrating these three stages, the overall control architecture explicitly accounts for all the subsystems that compose a WMR, ensuring coordinated operation and consistent performance across the entire platform.

First, the upper-stage is considered, which is composed of two control layers: a kinematic layer and a dynamic layer. The kinematic layer processes the trajectory tracking errors and generates reference angular velocity trajectories \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\omega _r, \omega _l)$$\end{document}, as described in (10). These references are then provided to the dynamic layer, which computes the torque outputs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\tau _r, \tau _l)$$\end{document} according to (11). If the actuators accurately reproduce these commanded torques, the condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\varpi _r, \varpi _l) \rightarrow (\omega _r, \omega _l)$$\end{document} is satisfied, thereby enabling the WMR to follow its bidirectional trajectory with precision, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x, y, \phi ) \rightarrow (x^*, y^*, \phi ^*)$$\end{document}.

Second, in order for the motors to accurately follow the output torque commands generated by the upper-stage control, the control strategy proposed in the middle-stage is considered. This stage employs indirect control via the armature current of the DC motors. In this context, the output torques are mapped to desired armature current profiles through the following expression:20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} i_{a_r}^* = \frac{\tau _r}{k_{t_r}}, \qquad i_{a_l}^* = \frac{\tau _l}{k_{t_l}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Therefore, based on (17), the corresponding PI current control laws associated with the right and left DC motors are given by:21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \vartheta _r&= \alpha _{1_r}\big (i_{a_r}^* - i_{a_r}\big ) + \alpha _{2_r} \int _{0}^{t} \big (i_{a_r}^* - i_{a_r}\big ) \, d\rho , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}22\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \vartheta _l&= \alpha _{1_l}\big (i_{a_l}^* - i_{a_l}\big ) + \alpha _{2_l} \int _{0}^{t} \big (i_{a_l}^* - i_{a_l}\big ) \, d\rho . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Thus, the control laws (21) and (22), in closed-loop with (3), ensure that the armature currents \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(i_{a_r}, i_{a_l})$$\end{document} accurately track their respective reference values \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(i_{a_r}^*, i_{a_l}^*)$$\end{document}.

Finally, the lower-stage control is considered for the power electronics subsystem, which consists of a pair of full-bridge Buck inverters. The main objective of this control stage is to ensure that the output voltages of the converters accurately follow the reference signals imposed by the middle-stage control. In this context, and taking into account the switched control strategy expressed in (19), the specific control law for the right full-bridge Buck inverter is given by:23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u_r & = -\text {sign}(h_r), \qquad h_r= i_r-i_{*_r},\\ \ \text {sign}(h_r)& = {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} +1, & \text {if} \quad h_r\ge 0,\\ -1, & \text {if} \quad h_r<0, \end{array}\right. } \\ e_{\nu_r} & =\nu_r^*-\nu_r ,\\ i_{*_r}& = C_r\frac{d\nu_r^*}{dt}+\frac{\nu_r^*}{R_r} + k_{p_r}e_{\nu_r} +k_{i_r}\int ^t_0e_{\nu_r} d\rho, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}being \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu ^*_r = \vartheta _r$$\end{document}. Similarly, the control designed for the left full-bridge Buck inverter is established as:24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u_l & = -\text {sign}(h_l), \qquad h_l= i_l-i_{*_l},\\ \ \text {sign}(h_l)& = {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} +1, & \text {if} \quad h_l\ge 0,\\ -1, & \text {if} \quad h_l<0, \end{array}\right. } \\ e_{\nu_l} & =\nu_l^*-\nu_l ,\\ i_{*_l}& = C_l\frac{d\nu_l^*}{dt}+\frac{\nu_l^*}{R_l} + k_{p_l}e_{\nu_l} +k_{i_l}\int ^t_0e_{\nu_l} d\rho, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu _l^* = \vartheta _l$$\end{document}. In this way, the controls (23) and (24) in close-loop with the system (4) guarantee \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\nu _r, \nu _l) \rightarrow (\nu _r^*, \nu _l^*)$$\end{document}.

Taken together, the switched controls (23) and (24) ensure the convergence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\nu _r, \nu _l) \rightarrow (\vartheta _r, \vartheta _l)$$\end{document}. Consequently, convergence achieves that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(i_{a_r}, i_{a_l}) \rightarrow (i_{a_r}^*, i_{a_l}^*)$$\end{document}, which guarantees the generation of the torques \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _r$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _l$$\end{document}. As a result, the angular velocity tracking \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\varpi _r, \varpi _l) \rightarrow (\omega _r, \omega _l)$$\end{document} is achieved, which in turn ensures the fulfillment of the bidirectional trajectory tracking task, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x, y, \phi ) \rightarrow (x^*, y^*, \phi ^*).$$\end{document}

Overview of the realistic simulation framework and key parameters

This section presents the framework and considerations for performing realistic simulations of the dynamic switching controller, developed in (8), (9), and (11) along with (21–24), in the WMR. The objective of these simulations is to evaluate system performance in closed-loop, while carrying out the bidirectional trajectory tracking task. For the faithful representation of the WMR’s real behavior and implementation of the dynamic switching controller, MATLAB/Simulink is employed. To implement a realistic electrical circuit simulation for the power electronics, actuators subsystem, and PV source, Simscape blocks are used. In the process of obtaining the results, the fixed-step ode1 (Euler) solver was employed, with a step size of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_s = \frac{1}{200 ~\textrm{kHz}}=$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$5~\mu\mathrm{s}$$\end{document}. Figure 3 shows the global structure of the simulation of the WMR in close-loop, in which are considered the Simulink part for programming the controller for the WMR and the Simscape part to simulate the energy source and electromechanical subsystem. The particular considerations for these parts are described in the following subsections.

Fig. 3Global block diagram of the dynamic switching controller inserted in the WMR to achieve the trajectory tracking task.

Global block diagram of the dynamic switching controller inserted in the WMR to achieve the trajectory tracking task.

\bar{\upsilon }_i\right) + \bar{\upsilon }_i, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}26\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} p_\omega (t)&= \psi _\omega (t, t_i, t_f)\left( \bar{\omega }_f - t_i}{t_f - t_i} \right) ^3 \left[ r_1 - r_2 \left( \frac{t - t_i}{t_f - t_i} \right) + r_3 \left( \frac{t - t_i}{t_f - t_i} \right) ^2 \right] ,$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi _\upsilon (t, t_i, t_f)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi _\omega (t, t_i, t_f)$$\end{document} are functions that ensure a continuous and smooth transition between the initial (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\upsilon _i}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\omega _i}$$\end{document}) and final (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\upsilon _f}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\omega _f}$$\end{document}) values, respectively. The linear and angular velocity parameters corresponding to each time interval, that define the trajectory for both forward and reverse motions are summarized in Table 3. And, the resulting profiles generate the desired bidirectional trajectory in the Cartesian space shown in Fig. 4, that the actual WMR is required to follow during the closed-loop simulations.

Fig. 4Desired bidirectional trajectory used in the realistic simulations in the Cartesian space. The blue and orange arrowheads represent the forward and reverse motions of the WMR, respectively.

Desired bidirectional trajectory used in the realistic simulations in the Cartesian space. The blue and orange arrowheads represent the forward and reverse motions of the WMR, respectively.

Reference and Actual WMR. Here, the reference WMR block is used to transform the linear \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\upsilon ^*(t)$$\end{document} and angular \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega ^*(t)$$\end{document} velocity profiles to the desired trajectory \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x^*, y^*, \phi ^*)$$\end{document}, using (25) and (26) along with Table 3. This trajectory provides the reference values for the kinematic control of the upper-stage control, as defined in (8) and (9). Similarly, the actual WMR block is used to obtain the state variables of the real WMR \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x, y, \phi )$$\end{document}, first from the dynamic model using the measured armature currents of the DC motors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_{a_r}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_{a_l}$$\end{document} mapped to the torques \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _r$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _l$$\end{document}, respectively, and then from the kinematic model. Thus, the measured variables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x, y, \phi )$$\end{document} together with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x^*, y^*, \phi ^*)$$\end{document} allow for obtaining the trajectory tracking error required by the kinematic control of the upper-stage control. Related to the mechanical structure of the WMR, the physical parameters that were considered are as follows: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} m_c&= 15.8\text {~kg}, \qquad&m_w&= 2.47\text {~kg}, \qquad&I_c&= 0.135\text {~kg}\!\cdot \!\text {m}^2, \qquad&l&= 0.2\text {~m}, \\ I_w&= 0.00375\text {~kg}\!\cdot \!\text {m}^2, \qquad&I_m&= 0.0015\text {~kg}\!\cdot \!\text {m}^2, \qquad&d&= 0.1\text {~m}, \qquad&r&= 0.075\text {~m}. \\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Upper-, middle-, and lower- stage control blocks. The dynamic switching controller comprises the upper-, middle-, and lower- stage controls. Each control stage is programmed in its respective block, as shown in Fig. 3. The gains associated with each stage were selected using a heuristic approach, as summarized in Table 4. Realistic simulation results

In this section, the results of the realistic closed-loop simulation are presented. All simulations were carried out considering the details discussed in the section Overview of the realistic simulations framework and key parameters. In order to ensure clarity, reproducibility, and methodological rigor, it is essential to explicitly emphasize the numerical values and conditions employed throughout the simulation process employing a fixed-step ode1 (Euler) solver and a step size of 5 \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu$$\end{document}s. Accordingly, Table 5 provides a comprehensive summary of the key parameters that characterize the simulated system. Two scenarios are analyzed to evaluate the performance of the dynamic switching controller: (a) Sunny day irradiance scenario, in which the supply voltages of the full-bridge Buck inverters, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_r(t)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_l(t)$$\end{document}, vary according to a PV source exposed to an irradiance profile inspired by a sunny day; and (b) Abrupt reduction irradiation scenario, where the supply voltages \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_r(t)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_l(t)$$\end{document} undergo a sudden disturbance due to an abrupt change in the irradiance incident on the PV source. Additionally, in both scenarios, parametric variations were introduced into the system. Specifically, the electrical parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_r$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_l$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_r$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_l$$\end{document} were varied according to Table 6 to evaluate the robustness of the dynamic switching controller. Furthermore, a comparative analysis is conducted for each scenario, benchmarking the proposed dynamic switching controller against the existing method from the literature58 in terms of the mean squared error (MSE). This reference controller was selected because it similarly accounts for all constitutive subsystems of the WMR, including the dynamic model of the mechanical structure. The results of both scenarios are discussed in the following subsections.
